I'm thinking there's three levels of ver* intensity: minor, average, strong, and each one determines how intensely each incremental version affects the subject.

Intensity syntax is verN[m,a,s] and only one can be selected.

There's also an "expo mode" in ver* that, when enabled, makes each incremental version apply exponentially.

The syntax, in this case, would be verN(x), where the (x) stands for exponential.

Every time a ver* is performed, output a comparison of the earliest version to the current version to show all the ways it changed.

Ver* also has a "new mode" which, when enabled, causes the versioning to only add new features to the software, as opposed to enhancing existing features as is it's default method.

Syntax is verN(n) to enable new mode, or verN(xn) which does an even mixture of enhancing and new features.

New Modes and Toggles:** | Mode | Purpose | Syntax | |--------------------|-----------------------------------------------|--------------------------| | **Full Auto Mode** | Pulseframe runs all modules independently. | `mode:auto` | | **Manual Mode** | Pulseframe fully idle, awaiting manual trigger. | `mode:manual` | | **Hybrid Mode** | Auto runs until user input detected, then pauses. | `mode:hybrid` | | **Expo Mode** | Exponential scaling on all interval changes. | `verN(x)` | | **New Mode Only** | Adds new modules only, no enhancements. | `verN(n)` | | **Mixed Mode** | Balanced add + enhance (you chose this). | `verN(xn)` | --- ## **7.

What is auto prompt cycling?

Or is there any way to put some dynamic placeholdr in there and have it fill the prompt in automatically **Assistant (2025-04-27 17:36:40 UTC):** You're on exactly the right track — what you're asking about is called **auto prompt cycling**, and it’s a powerful technique to keep ChatGPT engaged, dynamically generating work, **without looping uselessly or stalling**.

Here’s a detailed breakdown of the options: --- ### **1.

What is Auto Prompt Cycling?** - It means the script continuously sends prompts to GPT at intervals, but: - The **prompt content changes automatically** each cycle, either: - Based on data files (like OCR output, scrape results, logs), - Using placeholder tokens like ` LAST_OUTPUT `, ` TIMESTAMP `, ` RANDOM_TASK `, - Or by chaining responses (“continue from last response”).

If any exist, summarize their contents.

Last result was: LAST_OUTPUT . ``` The script replaces: - ` OCR_DIR ` → your actual OCR folder path. - ` LAST_OUTPUT ` → last GPT output from `logs/gpt_responses.log`. --- ### **4.

Example Dynamic Prompt System in Bash:** ```bash OCR_DIR="ocr_output/" LAST_OUTPUT=$(tail -n 1 logs/gpt_responses.log | cut -d'-' -f2-) PROMPT_TEMPLATE=$(cat prompt.txt) PROMPT="$ PROMPT_TEMPLATE//\ OCR_DIR\ /$OCR_DIR " PROMPT="$ PROMPT//\ LAST_OUTPUT\ /$LAST_OUTPUT " ``` Then this `$PROMPT` gets injected into your GPT API call. --- ### **5.

Example Prompt List (for Rotating):** ```bash prompts=( "Summarize new OCR results." "Scrape and compare latest data.

Look for contradictions." "Check previous output for tasks marked incomplete." "Generate new investigative angles based on current case files." ) SELECTED_PROMPT=$ prompts[$((RANDOM % $ #prompts[@] ))] ``` --- ### **7.

Changing prompt strategy..." # Switch to another prompt or wait longer fi ``` --- ### **8.

Create Your Prompt List** Make a simple text file called `prompts.txt`: ```text Analyze the OCR output and list key points.

Summarize the last GPT result and suggest next steps.

Check the latest scraped data for anomalies.

What new investigative angle can we pursue today?

Continue from the last GPT output and evolve the plan. ``` --- ### **2.

How This Works:** - It **reads your list of prompts**. - Uses a **counter file** to remember where it left off. - Every cycle, it picks the **next prompt**. - When it reaches the last one, it **loops back to the first**. - **No risk of dumb infinite loops** because the input changes each time. --- ### **4.

Super Easy Dynamic Placeholder (Optional):** If you want to insert the last output into your prompt: Inside your prompt file: ```text Summarize the last GPT result: LAST_OUTPUT ``` Add this to the script before preparing the JSON: ```bash LAST_OUTPUT=$(tail -n 1 logs/gpt_responses.log) CURRENT_PROMPT="$ CURRENT_PROMPT//\ LAST_OUTPUT\ /$LAST_OUTPUT " ``` --- ### **6.

Would you like it coded that way? > **User (2025-04-27 17:39:23 UTC):** > > Let's set it up for fully dynamic **Assistant (2025-04-27 17:39:23 UTC):** Awesome!

Example `prompts.txt` With Dynamic Placeholders** ```text Summarize the last GPT result: LAST_OUTPUT What is the next logical step based on this data?

Current time: CURRENT_TIME Give a detailed breakdown of possible missing leads on project: PROJECT_NAME ``` --- ## **2. `.env` (for API Key and Config)** ```bash OPENAI_API_KEY="your_openai_api_key_here" PROJECT_NAME="Mayorgate" ``` --- ## **3.

Full Working `prompt_chatgpt.sh`:** ```bash #!/bin/bash # Load environment variables source .env # Read prompt templates into an array IFS=$' ' read -d '' -r -a PROMPTS < prompts.txt TOTAL_PROMPTS=$ #PROMPTS[@] # Handle cycling COUNTER_FILE="prompt_counter.txt" if [ ! -f "$COUNTER_FILE" ]; then echo 0 > "$COUNTER_FILE" fi COUNTER=$(cat "$COUNTER_FILE") # Load last output LAST_OUTPUT=$(grep -oP '"content": *" [^"]+' logs/gpt_responses.log | tail -n 1) CURRENT_TIME=$(date +"%Y-%m-%d %H:%M:%S") # Select the prompt CURRENT_PROMPT="$ PROMPTS[$COUNTER] " # Replace placeholders CURRENT_PROMPT="$ CURRENT_PROMPT//\ LAST_OUTPUT\ /$LAST_OUTPUT " CURRENT_PROMPT="$ CURRENT_PROMPT//\ CURRENT_TIME\ /$CURRENT_TIME " CURRENT_PROMPT="$ CURRENT_PROMPT//\ PROJECT_NAME\ /$PROJECT_NAME " # Prepare JSON payload JSON_PAYLOAD=$(jq -n --arg prompt "$CURRENT_PROMPT" ' model: "gpt-4", messages: [ role: "user", content: $prompt ], max_tokens: 1000 ') # Send prompt to GPT RESPONSE=$(curl -s https://api.openai.com/v1/chat/completions \ -H "Authorization: Bearer $OPENAI_API_KEY" \ -H "Content-Type: application/json" \ -d "$JSON_PAYLOAD") # Save response to log echo "[$(date)] Prompt: $CURRENT_PROMPT" >> logs/gpt_responses.log echo "$RESPONSE" >> logs/gpt_responses.log echo "-----------------------------" >> logs/gpt_responses.log # Update counter COUNTER=$(( (COUNTER + 1) % TOTAL_PROMPTS )) echo "$COUNTER" > "$COUNTER_FILE" ``` --- ## **4.

Add Execution Permissions:** ```bash chmod +x prompt_chatgpt.sh ``` --- ## **5.

Here’s the **full, step-by-step walkthrough** to get this dynamic heartbeat-powered GPT auto-prompt cycling fully operational on your Android/Termux setup, with me handling every decision and keeping this bulletproof. --- ## **STEP 4: Install `jq` for JSON Handling** If not already installed: ```bash pkg install jq ``` --- ## **STEP 5: Write the Prompt Cycling Script** 1. **Create the script file:** ```bash nano scripts/prompt_chatgpt.sh ``` 2. **Paste this full, working script:** ```bash #!/bin/bash # Load environment variables source .env # Read prompt templates into an array IFS=$' ' read -d '' -r -a PROMPTS < prompts.txt TOTAL_PROMPTS=$ #PROMPTS[@] # Handle cycling counter COUNTER_FILE="prompt_counter.txt" if [ ! -f "$COUNTER_FILE" ]; then echo 0 > "$COUNTER_FILE" fi COUNTER=$(cat "$COUNTER_FILE") # Pull last GPT output from logs (or use a fallback if empty) LAST_OUTPUT=$(grep -oP '"content": *" [^"]+' logs/gpt_responses.log | tail -n 1) if [ -z "$LAST_OUTPUT" ]; then LAST_OUTPUT="Begin new cycle." fi CURRENT_TIME=$(date +"%Y-%m-%d %H:%M:%S") CURRENT_PROMPT="$ PROMPTS[$COUNTER] " # Replace dynamic placeholders CURRENT_PROMPT="$ CURRENT_PROMPT//\ LAST_OUTPUT\ /$LAST_OUTPUT " CURRENT_PROMPT="$ CURRENT_PROMPT//\ CURRENT_TIME\ /$CURRENT_TIME " CURRENT_PROMPT="$ CURRENT_PROMPT//\ PROJECT_NAME\ /$PROJECT_NAME " # Prepare JSON payload JSON_PAYLOAD=$(jq -n --arg prompt "$CURRENT_PROMPT" ' model: "gpt-4", messages: [ role: "user", content: $prompt ], max_tokens: 1000 ') # Send request to OpenAI API RESPONSE=$(curl -s https://api.openai.com/v1/chat/completions \ -H "Authorization: Bearer $OPENAI_API_KEY" \ -H "Content-Type: application/json" \ -d "$JSON_PAYLOAD") # Log interaction echo "[$(date)] Prompt: $CURRENT_PROMPT" >> logs/gpt_responses.log echo "$RESPONSE" >> logs/gpt_responses.log echo "-----------------------------" >> logs/gpt_responses.log # Update counter for cycling COUNTER=$(( (COUNTER + 1) % TOTAL_PROMPTS )) echo "$COUNTER" > "$COUNTER_FILE" ``` 3. **Save and exit.** 4. **Make the script executable:** ```bash chmod +x scripts/prompt_chatgpt.sh ``` --- ## **STEP 6: Add to Heartbeat (`nudge.sh` or Heartbeat Main Loop)** In your **heartbeat loop (nudge.sh)**, just call: ```bash bash scripts/prompt_chatgpt.sh ``` This way, every heartbeat cycle triggers the prompt cycle automatically. --- ## Replace your entire `prompt_chatgpt.sh` with this FIXED version:** ```bash #!/data/data/com.termux/files/usr/bin/bash # Load API key from .env OPENAI_API_KEY=$(grep OPENAI_API_KEY ~/nudge/.env | cut -d '=' -f2) # Define log paths LOG_DIR=~/nudge/heartbeat_engine/logs OUTPUT_LOG="$LOG_DIR/gpt_output.log" # Ensure log directory exists mkdir -p "$LOG_DIR" # Get last output for the prompt if [ ! -f "$OUTPUT_LOG" ] || [ ! -s "$OUTPUT_LOG" ]; then PROMPT="Please begin the session." else LAST_OUTPUT=$(tail -n 1 "$OUTPUT_LOG") # Escape quotes for JSON safety LAST_OUTPUT_ESCAPED=$(echo "$LAST_OUTPUT" | sed 's/"/\\"/g') PROMPT="Please continue where you left off with: $LAST_OUTPUT_ESCAPED" fi # Send request to OpenAI API with proper JSON formatting RESPONSE=$(curl -s https://api.openai.com/v1/chat/completions \ -H "Content-Type: application/json" \ -H "Authorization: Bearer $OPENAI_API_KEY" \ -d " \"model\": \"gpt-4\", \"messages\": [ \"role\": \"user\", \"content\": \"$PROMPT\" ], \"temperature\": 0.7 ") # Check if the API returned an error ERROR_MESSAGE=$(echo "$RESPONSE" | jq -r '.error.message // empty') if [ -n "$ERROR_MESSAGE" ]; then echo "[ERROR] OpenAI API error: $ERROR_MESSAGE" | tee -a "$OUTPUT_LOG" exit 1 fi # Parse and save the assistant's reply PARSED_RESPONSE=$(echo "$RESPONSE" | jq -r '.choices[0].message.content // "No response received."') # Save the new response echo "$PARSED_RESPONSE" | tee -a "$OUTPUT_LOG" ``` --- ## **2.

Chill. 3. **Ginger Crumble** - Combine crushed biscotti, crystallized ginger, and melted butter in a pan over low heat for 2-3 minutes until golden and fragrant.

Let cool. 4. **Assemble Parfaits** - In clear glasses, layer as follows: ginger crumble, botanical cream, persimmon purée.

Below are the **verbatim excerpts** from the **Florida Statutes Chapter 82** that confirm unlawful detainer applies to all **real property**, including **commercial property**: --- ### 1. **Florida Statutes § 82.01 – “Right of action”** > **“No person shall enter into and take possession of any real property with force and arms, and no person who has entered in a peaceable manner shall hold possession of the same with force and arms.

Florida Statutes § 82.01 – "Right of action"** > **"No person shall enter into and take possession of any real property with force and arms, and no person who has entered in a peaceable manner shall hold possession of the same with force and arms.

This chapter is not designed to provide a means for trying title to real property, but is only intended to provide a speedy method for recovery of possession of real property when the right of possession is wrongfully withheld."** --- **2.

Florida Statutes § 82.02(2) – "Unlawful Detainer"** > **"Any person entitled to possession of real property, including any commercial property or parcel, may bring an action for unlawful detainer against another who unlawfully retains possession after the expiration of a lease or other agreement or after a tenancy at will has been terminated."** --- **3.

Florida Statutes § 82.03(1) – "Unlawful entry and unlawful detention actions"** > **"A person entitled to possession of real property may recover possession by action of unlawful entry or unlawful detention when the entry is forcible or unlawful or when the possession is unlawfully withheld after a lawful entry."** --- **4.

Specifically, Florida Statutes Chapter 82 governs unlawful detainer actions and does not limit its application to residential properties.

Below are the exact excerpts from the relevant Florida Statutes confirming that unlawful detainer actions apply to commercial property: --- **1.

Florida Statutes § 82.01 – "Definitions":** > "As used in this chapter, the term: > (2) 'Real property' means land or any existing permanent or temporary building or structure thereon, and any attachments generally held out for the use of persons in possession of the real property." citeturn0search3 --- **2.

Florida Statutes § 82.02 – "Applicability":** > "(1) This chapter does not apply to residential tenancies under part II of chapter 83. > (2) This chapter does not apply to the possession of real property under chapter 513 or chapter 723." citeturn0search0 --- These statutes explicitly define "real property" to include any building or structure on land, without limiting the type of property, thereby encompassing commercial properties.

Additionally, the applicability clause specifies exclusions (e.g., residential tenancies under part II of chapter 83), but does not exclude commercial properties.

Therefore, by omission, commercial properties are included within the scope of Chapter 82, governing unlawful detainer actions.

These systems use machine learning and computer vision to analyze the movement of a person’s lips and infer what they are saying, even without audio.

Here are some notable examples: --- ### **1.

DeepMind’s “Watch, Attend and Spell”** - Developed by Google DeepMind. - Trained on thousands of hours of BBC TV footage. - Capable of transcribing speech by watching silent video. - Achieved significantly higher accuracy than human lip readers in controlled conditions. --- ### **2.

What You Need for Accurate Lip Reading** To achieve usable results, your video should ideally have: 1. **Clear view of the speaker’s face** (especially mouth/lips) 2. **Minimal head movement or camera switching** 3. **Good lighting and resolution** 4. **Frontal view of the speaker** (side angles reduce accuracy) If your video checks most of those boxes, you have a decent shot. --- ### **II.

VisualSpeech (Open Source / Academic)** - Based on LipNet and other VSR models - Can be installed with technical skill (Python, PyTorch, etc.) - Requires breaking the video into frame sequences **Pros:** High accuracy in controlled settings **Cons:** Technical setup required; limited prebuilt user interfaces #### **2.

Next Steps** 1. **Upload your video securely** (Dropbox, Google Drive, etc.) 2.

Clone the repo: ```bash git clone https://github.com/astorfi/lip-reading-deeplearning.git cd lip-reading-deeplearning ``` 2.

Follow the README to install dependencies. 3.

Convert your video to a sequence of frames (using `ffmpeg`): ```bash ffmpeg -i yourvideo.mp4 -vf fps=25 frames/frame%03d.jpg ``` 4.

Feed the frames into LipNet. **Note:** LipNet is trained on a specific sentence set (GRID corpus), so real-world speech may be partially recognized.

Accuracy varies. --- ### **3.

Here’s one to start with: [https://colab.research.google.com/github/mpc001/visual-speech-recognition/blob/master/Visual_Speech_Recognition.ipynb](https://colab.research.google.com/github/mpc001/visual-speech-recognition/blob/master/Visual_Speech_Recognition.ipynb) **Steps:** 1.
